-6.36]* | -4.4 [-10.0, +1.1] | 4/6/20 | \nTwo data caveats apply across methods. First, a run with no valid submission is\nimputed at the worst possible percentile rather than dropped (Section [5.1](#S5.SS1)’s\nconvention); this creates exact ties between two very different failure modes and gives a\nhandful of large-magnitude gaps outsized leverage on every method’s point estimate or test\nstatistic. Second, seed counts per task can be uneven, which the per-task-mean-based\nmethods treat as equally reliable regardless of count.\nD.3 Tuning effort invested in baseline harnesses\nBecause Malena is our own system, a natural concern is that we invested asymmetrically\nmore integration and tuning effort into it than into the external harnesses it is compared\nagainst. This appendix reports that effort, spanning backbone-compatibility bug fixes and\ntargeted configuration ablations on MLEvolve, ScienceFlow, and Arbor. In each case, this\nwork either (i) fixed a genuine defect — adopted into every canonical batch reported\nelsewhere in the paper; (ii) found no reliable improvement, in which case we kept the\nharness’s original canonical configuration and report the negative result here rather than\nomitting it; or (iii) found a genuine backbone-specific configuration effect and adopted the\nbetter setting as canonical — the one such case is MLEvolve’s reasoning effort on\nDeepSeek V4 Flash (Appendix [D.3](#A4.SS3.SSS0.Px1)).\nMLEvolve: reasoning effort on DeepSeek V4 Flash.\nFor every other backbone reported above, MLEvolve’s canonical batches use that backbone’s own reasoning_effort=max (or equivalent) setting, matching every other method’s convention. On DeepSeek V4 Flash, we instead adopt reasoning_effort=medium as MLEvolve’s canonical setting, the only backbone where it departs from max. The reason is a length effect specific to this backbone: at max, DeepSeek V4 Flash emits substantially longer reasoning traces than it does at medium. Because MLEvolve’s tree search issues many independent, short LLM calls rather than growing a single session, per-call reasoning length translates almost directly into wall-clock cost per search step, so under max’s much longer traces MLEvolve completes only a fraction of the search steps it completes at medium within a fixed 24h budget.\nTo quantify this we compare max and medium on an identical 10-task subset\n(same tasks, same seed range, same 24h timeout for both arms; Table [17](#A4.T17)).\nCutting reasoning effort to medium reduces the median reasoning length per LLM call\nby roughly 5\\(\\times\\) (37,124 to 7,069 characters), which more than triples the mean number\nof completed search-tree nodes per run (18.5 to 62.8). Per-node quality is essentially\nunchanged between the two settings — the buggy-node rate is within noise (61% vs. 62%)\n— so the additional search volume at medium is not, on average, lower-quality than\nmax’s, just far more numerous. That additional search volume alone is enough to move\noutcomes substantially: any-medal rate rises from 0% to 25.0% (95% CI [10.0, 30.0]) and mean\npercentile from 42.6 (95% CI [36.0, 48.5]) to 53.1 (95% CI [44.9, 59.8]).\n| reasoning_effort | Any-medal rate (%) \\(\\uparrow\\) [95% CI] | None-rate (%) \\(\\downarrow\\) | Mean percentile \\(\\uparrow\\) [95% CI] | Median reasoning (chars) \\(\\downarrow\\) | Mean nodes/run \\(\\uparrow\\) | Buggy rate (%) \\(\\downarrow\\) | \n|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|\n| max | 0.0 [0.0, 0.0] | 13.8 | 42.6 [36.0, 48.5] | 37124 | 18.5 | 61 | \n| medium (canonical) | 25.0 [10.0, 30.0] | 2.5 | 53.1 [44.9, 59.8] | 7069 | 62.8 | 62 | \nIndependent tool-calling failures on GLM 5.2.\nBefore either harness’s canonical GLM 5.2 batch could run, we found and fixed two unrelated bugs that both manifested as tool-calling failures specific to this backbone. MLEvolve’s structured-output calls request a named (forced) tool_choice; GLM 5.2 served behind our vLLM deployment enters grammar-constrained decoding under a forced choice, and its FSM has no transition for the reasoning tokens the model wants to emit before its answer, so every such call failed with an internal server error. We fixed this by defaulting MLEvolve’s structured-output calls to tool_choice=auto (configurable per stage, so backends that do handle a forced choice reliably are unaffected). Independently, ScienceFlow’s LLM client always included a tools/tool_choice field in its request payload, even for calls with no tools to offer (e.g., its metric-output-interpreter step); the same GLM 5.2 deployment rejects an explicit empty tools: [] array outright, which we fixed by omitting the field entirely rather than sending an empty array. These are two independent root causes, each surfaced by validating against a live endpoint before launching the canonical batch, not two symptoms of the same underlying fix.\nScienceFlow: worker count and resource-admission policy.\nScienceFlow’s canonical configuration runs multiple parallel exploratory workers sharing a single GPU (2 workers for GLM 5.2; a single worker gated by an LLM-based resource-admission heuristic for DeepSeek V4 Flash). Suspecting that this concurrency could itself be a source of resource congestion, we tested two independent mitigations, one per backbone.\nOn GLM 5.2, we compared 1 worker against the canonical 2 workers across the full\nfixed30 split (Table [18](#A4.T18)). Any-medal\nrate and mean percentile are statistically indistinguishable between the two worker counts;\nthe 1-worker point estimate is nominally higher on any-medal rate (43.9% vs. 40.0%). This\ncomparison uses the full fixed30 split, following an inconclusive result on an earlier\n11-task subset; CIs at this sample size cannot rule out a modest true effect. We kept the\ncanonical 2-worker configuration.\nOn DeepSeek V4 Flash, we replaced ScienceFlow’s canonical resource_smart_llm\nadmission mode — an LLM-based admission heuristic plus a main-agent advisory signal that\ndecides when a worker may start a new GPU job — with the static rule-based\nresource_smart_policy, on the same canonical 10-task ablation subset used by\nAppendix [D.3](#A4.SS3.SSS0.Px1)’s MLEvolve ablation, to check whether the added\ncomplexity of the canonical policy was necessary (Table [19](#A4.T19)). It\nwas not: any-medal rate and mean percentile are both somewhat lower under\nresource_smart_policy, and the gap is within the noise we see elsewhere at this sample size. We kept\nresource_smart_llm as canonical.\n[D.3](#A4.SS3.SSS0.Px1)’s MLEvolve ablation. Both any-medal and mean percentile are oracle (best-of-pool) across every candidate ScienceFlow harvests per run, flipped so higher is better, matching every other percentile column in this paper. 95% CIs are bootstrapped over per-task run lists (2000 resamples), matching the convention used for the paper’s headline tables (Section\n[5.1](#S5.SS1)).\n| Configuration | Any-medal rate (%) \\(\\uparrow\\) [95% CI] | Gold (%) | Silver (%) | Bronze (%) | Mean percentile \\(\\uparrow\\) [95% CI] | \n|---|---|---|---|---|---|\n| resource_smart_llm (canonical) | 28.3 [10.0, 50.0] | 10.0 | 13.3 | 5.0 | 66.6 [52.9, 80.7] | \n| resource_smart_policy | 20.0 [0.0, 40.0] | 2.5 | 10.0 | 7.5 | 52.2 [39.6, 65.5] | \nArbor: executor timeout and convergence early-stopping (GLM 5.2).\nArbor’s canonical\nconfiguration for both GLM 5.2 and DeepSeek V4 Flash uses reasoning_effort=max, a\n1M-token context window, 3 parallel executors, and a 4-hour timeout per individual experiment\ninside its 24h wall-clock budget; the executor timeout and the stop_after=8\nconvergence early-stopping threshold ablated below are not values we chose — both are\nported unmodified from the mle_kaggle competition-optimization plugin bundled in\nArbor’s own public GitHub repository, so these ablations test Arbor’s own maintainers’\ndefaults rather than a configuration we picked ourselves. We ran two targeted ablations\nagainst this configuration. First, we tripled the executor timeout to\n12 hours; the medal rate is\nunchanged (Table [20](#A4.T20)). Second, we tried relaxing\nArbor’s convergence-based early stopping (default stop_after=8 consecutive\nnon-improving idea-tree expansions) to stop_after=999, effectively disabling it so a\nrun keeps exploring until its wall-clock budget runs out rather than stopping early: medal rate\nis again unchanged within noise\n(Table [20](#A4.T20)).\n| Ablation | Baseline any-medal rate (%) \\(\\uparrow\\) [95% CI] | Ablation any-medal rate (%) \\(\\uparrow\\) [95% CI] | \n|---|---|---|\n| Executor timeout: 4h \\(\\to\\) 12h | 12.5 [0.0, 50.0] | 12.5 [0.0, 50.0] | \n| Convergence early-stop: stop_after 8 \\(\\to\\) 999 | 50.0 [0.0, 100.0] | 43.8 [25.0, 75.0] | \nD.4 Trajectory and token usage with Kimi K3\nFigure [3](#S5.F3)’s two panels are GLM 5.2 only. Figure [9](#A4.F9) reproduces\nboth at Kimi K3 using the same fixed30 task set.\nThe overall shape matches Figure [3](#S5.F3): quality rises quickly in the\nfirst few hours and plateaus thereafter, with AiScientist again the slowest to converge. On\ncost, Malena’s cache-read volume again dominates its token footprint the same way it does\nat GLM 5.2, but Kimi K3’s much higher output pricing ($13.99/M vs. GLM 5.2’s $3.123/M)\nmakes MLEvolve’s uncached, output-heavy tree search the most expensive of the four shown\nhere.\nD.5 Quality by task difficulty\nIn order to better understand the strengths of Malena against other baselines, and better investigate its improved performance, we partition the fixed30 split into four different clusters, according to perceived difficulty among all harnesses:\n- 1. \nnomad2018, plant, spooky, stanford, us\n- 2. \naptos2019, freesound, h&m, hotel, hubmap, mlsp, tweet, whale\n- 3. \nbillion-word, cassava, imet, kuzushiji, multi-modal, nfl, petfinder\n- 4. \nalaska2, champs, hms, jigsaw, new, osic, smartphone, tensorflow2, uw, ventilator\nWe plot the individual oracle performance among these clusters in Figure [10](#A4.F10).\nOn the easiest cluster (1), all methods approach ceiling performance by 24h, though AiScientist lags behind for much of the run before catching up.\nOn cluster 2, Malena and MLEvolve maintain a sustained edge over Arbor and AiScientist through 24h.\nMalena performs significantly better than all other baselines on cluster 3, its clearest lead of the four clusters, suggesting a sweet spot between tasks that are inherently difficult for current AI agents and tasks that are comparatively easy.\nOn the hardest cluster (4), Malena keeps a smaller but consistent edge over the other three, which track closely together.\nD.6 NatureBench: evaluation protocol\nFor NatureBench, we follow the benchmark’s own online evaluation protocol as published\n(Appendix [A.3](#A1.SS3)): rather than a single held-out grader run once at the end\nof a run, as in MLE-bench, each task exposes a live /evaluate HTTP endpoint that an\nagent may query an unbounded number of times over the course of a run, with the endpoint\nautomatically tracking the best score seen across all calls. We give every harness we\ncompare here — Malena, AiScientist, and MLEvolve — unrestricted access to this\nendpoint, matching NatureBench’s protocol as published.\nWe extend the per-run time budget to 8h, double NatureBench’s own published default of 4h, to give the slower of our search-based baselines (MLEvolve, whose tree search advances through many short, sequential steps rather than one long session) a more comfortable margin within which to complete meaningful search.\nBecause the harness’s own protocol reports the best score achieved anywhere in the run —\nan oracle score, as described in Appendix [A.3](#A1.SS3) — rather than a\nself-selected final submission, this metric implicitly rewards however many independent\nattempts a harness manages to get graded within the time budget, on top of whatever quality\ngains its search process makes. Table [21](#A4.T21) reports the number of\n/evaluate calls per run for each harness, across our full NatureBench run cohort.\nMalena and AiScientist call the endpoint at a broadly similar rate\n(median 16 and 18 calls per run, respectively); MLEvolve calls it substantially less often\n(median 8 calls per run). Since a benefit under an oracle-best-of-run metric scales with the\nnumber of independent scoring opportunities exercised, this asymmetry may put MLEvolve at a\nmechanical disadvantage on this particular metric, independent of any difference in the\nquality of the strategies it discovers.\n|  | Malena | AiScientist | MLEvolve | \n|---|---|---|---|\n| p25 | 8 | 7 | 2 | \n| p50 (median) | 16 | 18 | 8 | \n| p75 | 36 | 96 | 18 | \nAppendix E Trace Analysis\nThis appendix gives the full methodology behind Section [5.2](#S5.SS2)’s checkpoint\nlabeling and the technique-overlap analysis it enables, including the labeling\nschema, the offline deduplication/clustering procedure, and technique-name\nnormalization we omitted from the main text for space. Labeling was run on a fixed set of\n10 original MLE-bench tasks (aptos2019, billion-word-imputation,\ncassava, champs, freesound, h&m, jigsaw,\nnfl, petfinder, ventilator) – not to be confused with the\nfixed10 challenging split used elsewhere in this paper, which is a different\nset of 10 tasks. The checkpoint-mining and\nlabeling steps below (through canonicalization) are the current pipeline behind\nFigure [5](#S5.F5) and\nFigure [4](#S5.F4). Unless stated otherwise, the\nlabeling/judge model throughout is GLM 5.2.\nE.1 Checkpoint mining\nCheckpoints. We unify run traces across methods to a common checkpoint format: a snapshot of the run’s code repository at specific points in time, method-dependent since each harness structures its own trajectory differently – for Malena, immediately before a long job submission, a long bash tool call, or a submission-registration tool call; for MLEvolve, before executing a candidate; for AiScientist, before a long bash tool call; for Arbor, before a job-submit call. Oneshot and Completions have no intermediate trajectory to snapshot and are labeled once, on the final state of the repository. Runs with multiple checkpoints are labeled sequentially along this chain, each job seeded with the entries already logged for its nearest earlier checkpoint so it only has to add, remove, or revise entries rather than re-deriving the whole technique set from scratch.\nLabeling schema (pass 1: technique extraction). The judge (GLM 5.2) reads a checkpoint’s code and logs zero or more entries, each with: a free-form snake_case name (no reuse-search against prior entries – naming convergence is handled later, offline); 1–3 categories, ordered most-to-least applicable, drawn from a fixed 13-way taxonomy (data_preprocessing, data_pipeline, feature_engineering, augmentation, architecture, loss, regularization, optimization, training, ensembling, model_selection, post_processing, inference, plus an other escape hatch (used for only 0.02% of all logged category-tag annotations across our labeled checkpoints), which we exclude from the category-share figures throughout this section); and three separate text fields instead of one blob – mechanism (task-agnostic, roughly 40 tokens, describing only WHAT IS COMPUTED, stripped of domain nouns and hyperparameters, the only field ever embedded for similarity/dedup), instantiation (task-specific detail, hyperparameters, and domain nouns, kept for search but never embedded), and evidence_sig (a 1–3 line canonical code excerpt for human/reviewer sanity-checking). Splitting mechanism from instantiation this way removes a specific noise source found in an earlier embedding-similarity audit, where chunk embeddings picked up code-context co-occurrence (e.g. dropout/mlp/layer_norm clustering together purely because they appear near each other in a forward pass) rather than technique identity.\nDeduplication and canonicalization (offline, per task). All identity resolution – deciding whether two independently-logged entries describe the same underlying technique – happens offline, never at labeling time, and entirely within one task’s own runs (never pooled across tasks, so no task’s vocabulary is built from or reviewed against another task’s runs). Pass-1 entries are embedded on their mechanism text using Qwen3-Embedding-8B and grouped by complete-linkage hierarchical clustering at a fixed cosine-similarity threshold (0.70, picked by sweeping method \\(\\times\\) threshold against a hand-audited corpus for cluster-size and same-run-duplicate violations). An automated GLM 5.2 review then runs in two phases: a split-review phase flags multi-member clusters with no category shared by every member and asks GLM 5.2 whether any member should be extracted into its own cluster; a merge-review phase takes every singleton cluster and asks the judge whether it is really the same technique as one of its nearest neighbors by mechanism-embedding similarity. A final canonicalization pass has GLM 5.2 synthesize one canonical name and mechanism description per cluster that a reader would recognize as covering every member’s variant, rather than mechanically picking one member’s text. Pass 2 then re-labels every run a second time, this time against the finalized, canonical cluster set: the judge calls log_known_technique(cluster_id, ...) directly when a checkpoint’s technique matches an existing cluster, and free-form entries otherwise, so a pass-2 match is never subject to a separate, error-prone post-hoc nearest-centroid assignment step.\nE.2 Main-text plot methodology\nThis subsection gives the exact construction behind the two main-text plots built on top\nof the canonical technique clusters above: the category-share-decay curves\n(Figure [5](#S5.F5)) and the per-technique rarity distribution\n(Figure [4](#S5.F4)).\nCategory-share decay (half-life). Figure [5](#S5.F5)\ntracks a method’s technique-category composition as a function of checkpoint\nfraction \\(x\\in[0,1]\\) (progress through a run, pooled over every checkpoint of every run\nof that method). Every technique discovered at fraction \\(f\\leq x\\) contributes weight\n\\(0.5^{(x-f)/h}\\) to \\(x\\)’s composition, for half-life \\(h\\): a technique discovered exactly at\n\\(x\\) has full weight 1, one discovered \\(h\\) earlier has weight 0.5, and older ones decay\nsmoothly toward (but never reach) 0. Weighted category-tag counts are normalized to shares\nat each \\(x\\). \\(h\\to\\infty\\) recovers a plain cumulative count of every technique discovered\nso far; \\(h\\to 0\\) recovers an immediate, local snapshot of only the most recently discovered\ntechniques. The main text uses \\(h=0.25\\); Appendix [E.3](#A5.SS3)\nbelow checks smaller half-lives and per-task (non-pooled) curves.\nTechnique rarity.\nFigure [4](#S5.F4) reports, per method,\nthe distribution of how rare the canonical technique clusters that method’s runs exhibit\nare. Rarity is computed independently per task, not globally: for a fixed task, pool\nevery run of every (method, backbone) group on that task, and for each cluster \\(c\\) let \\(n_{c}\\)\nbe how many of those pooled runs exhibit it; \\(c\\)’s rarity is \\(1-n_{c}/n_{\\text{total}}\\), the\nfraction of that task’s pooled runs that did not use it. Pooling within the task\nacross every group (rather than, say, comparing a group only to one fixed baseline) means\nrarity reflects how many runs overall exhibit a technique, not how many distinct\ngroups do – a technique used by only a handful of runs stays rare even when those runs\nhappen to span more than one group (Appendix [E.5](#A5.SS5) gives a\nrarity-0.9 example shared across exactly two groups).\nFigure [4](#S5.F4) plots, per method, the resulting distribution of\nthese per-technique rarity values (pooled across backbones and 10 tasks) into\nsix windows centered at rarity \\(0,0.2,\\ldots,1.0\\) (half-width \\(0.1\\)).\nE.3 Robustness of the category-share-decay curves\nFigure [5](#S5.F5) in the main text pools all\nlabeled tasks at a single half-life (0.25). Figure [11](#A5.F11)\nshows the same pooled construction at three half-lives closer to the main\ntext’s value (0.1, 0.15, 0.2), and\nFigures [12](#A5.F12) and [13](#A5.F13)\nshow the identical three-half-life construction on two individual tasks\n(aptos2019 and nfl)\ninstead of pooled, to check that the shape of Malena’s late-run shift toward\nensembling/model_selection is not an artifact of the particular half-life or\nof pooling across tasks.\nE.4 Per-task technique-category composition across methods\nFigure [5](#S5.F5) and\nFigure [4](#S5.F4) both look at a method’s technique\nchoices pooled across all 10 tasks – respectively how its category mix\nshifts over the course of a run, and how rare its techniques are relative to\nevery other group. A complementary question is whether methods reach for a\ndifferent mix of technique categories than each other on the\nsame task, i.e. whether task identity or method identity is the\nbigger driver of what a method tries. Here we look directly at the\ntechnique-category composition of each method’s repertoire on each task\nindependently.\nFor a given (task, method) pair, we take the set of every distinct canonical\ntechnique cluster (Appendix [E.1](#A5.SS1) above) any of that\nmethod’s backbone/seed runs on that task exhibit, and tally, for each\ncategory tag in the taxonomy above, how many of those clusters carry it (a\ncluster tagged with 2 categories contributes to both – categories are not\nsplit fractionally, so the resulting bar shows a share of category-tags,\nnot of techniques). This is a presence-based snapshot (did the method ever\ntry a category-tagged technique at all over the course of the run), not\nusage-frequency-weighted like Figure [5](#S5.F5)’s\ndecay curves – deliberately simpler, since here we only need one static\ncomposition profile per (task, method) rather than a time series.\nFigure [14](#A5.F14) shows the resulting composition\nfor all 10 tasks. Two things stand out. First, task identity is a strong\ndriver on its own: feature_engineering dominates every method’s\nprofile on champs,\nh&m, and\nnfl, while ensembling dominates on\npetfinder and, for Malena and AiScientist\nspecifically, on aptos2019,\ncassava, and\njigsaw as well. Second,\non top of that shared task-driven baseline, Malena and AiScientist’s\nprofiles track each other closely on most tasks (e.g. aptos2019,\ncassava,\nfreesound,\nnfl), while MLEvolve is the most frequent\noutlier – visibly heavier on data_preprocessing and lighter on\nensembling on several of those same tasks, consistent with its\npooled under-representation of ensembling growth in\nFigure [5](#S5.F5).\nThe task jigsaw shows the largest\ncross-method spread: Oneshots’ profile there is missing most of the\nensembling share the other three methods have, replaced by a much\nlarger loss/optimization block.\nE.5 Illustrative session excerpts\nThe excerpts below are verbatim (only trimmed for length, marked with ...) pulls\nfrom real session transcripts, given as illustrative examples of the reasoning/tool-call\nstructure behind the checkpoint-labeling claims in Section [5.2](#S5.SS2). To break this stagnation, we must apply a Tier 2 shift focusing on robust regularization rather than just tweaking losses or backbones. By introducing Mixup data augmentation, stochastic depth (Drop Path), and feature-level dropout, we force the model to learn smoother, generalized decision boundaries between severity grades, effectively combatting single-landscape overfitting. </reasoning>\nExample 3: AiScientist, GLM 5.2, aptos2019.\n<reasoning> Now I have a clear picture. [truncated in log] -> [tool_result] result_len=1233 (stdout not recoverable in this log format)\nThis is the final record of the subagent’s session (30 total records): after inspecting all 8 trained models’ summaries/histories over 9 prior turns (git log, config/dataset/model/utils reads, per-model kfold summaries, training histories, GPU/torch versions), it explicitly frames the decision as “no broad rewrite” and chooses an ensemble-reweighting analysis over artifacts/ensemble_kfold_oof.csv rather than editing any training code (14 bash + 6 read_file_chunk calls, 0 edit_file calls across the whole subagent session).\nExample 4: Malena, GLM 5.2, whale –\nthe two-stage pipeline referenced in Section [5.2](#S5.SS2).\nWithin its first 17 checkpoints, Malena settles on a two-stage pipeline and\nnever changes its architecture again over the remaining 63 (80 total): a\nmetric-learning embedder, followed by nearest-neighbor retrieval with a\nlearned novelty detector.\n<reasoning> (checkpoint 000) ...Compute embeddings for all training images and test images... find nearest training images... Insert new_whale based on threshold... Use validation set to find optimal threshold. </reasoning>\nStage 1 (src/train.py) trains a CNN/ViT backbone with an ArcFace loss to produce normalized whale-fluke embeddings. Stage 2 (src/retrieve.py, src/centroid_submit.py, src/detector.py) computes cosine-similarity nearest neighbors against the training embeddings, ranks top-5 candidates via per-class centroids, and uses a small logistic-regression classifier over similarity-gap features (top-1/2/3 similarity, gaps, top-5 mean/std) to flag new_whale queries – individuals never seen in training. All 63 subsequent checkpoints are targeted patches to this fixed pipeline rather than new stages: backbone swaps (EfficientNetV2 \\(\\to\\) ConvNeXt \\(\\to\\) Swin), input-resolution bumps, novelty-threshold retuning, and ensembling. One checkpoint (014_ms_centroid_fAB) illustrates the granularity of these patches:\n<reasoning> (checkpoint 014_ms_centroid_fAB) ...The gap between 0.663 and 0.722 oracle is the new-detection error. A better new-detector could use... but let me first improve the embedding... </reasoning>\nThat is, the agent is choosing which fixed sub-module of the pipeline to improve next, at the granularity of a targeted code change, rather than tuning a numeric hyperparameter or introducing a new pipeline stage.\nExample 5: independent convergence on soft-label self-training,\naptos2019 (GLM 5.2 vs. Kimi K3).\nMalena’s GLM 5.2 and Kimi K3 runs on aptos2019\nindependently converge on the same canonical technique cluster – soft-label\nself-training (pseudo-labeling using an ensemble’s own predictions as soft\ntargets) – without sharing a run, then diverge in how much surrounding\nmachinery each backbone builds around it. This cluster has rarity 0.9\n(Appendix [E.2](#A5.SS2)): across every AiScientist, MLEvolve, and\nOneshot run on this task, none use it.\nThe GLM 5.2 run (logged as pseudo_label_self_training):\nMechanism: Generate soft pseudo-labels by running a trained model on unlabeled data, combine the pseudo-labeled samples with the original labeled training set (using soft target distributions and a per-sample weight for pseudo-labeled examples), and retrain the model on the augmented dataset. Instantiation: dataset.py PseudoDataset combines train images (hard labels, soft-onehot with label smoothing) and pseudo-labeled test images (soft probs from ensemble predictions). train_pl.py orchestrates per-fold pseudo-label training. Pseudo-labels sourced from infer.py --save_test_probs output.\nThe Kimi K3 run (logged independently as self_training_pseudo_labels), landing on the identical mechanism:\nInstantiation: train_pseudo.py loads ensemble test probability predictions (from compute_ensemble_test_probs.py) as soft pseudo-labels for unlabeled test images. Trains jointly on labeled train data (one-hot hard targets) and pseudo-labeled test data (soft 5-class probs) via soft cross-entropy... Pseudo data influence controlled by --pseudo_repeat (dataset repetition factor)... Supports per-fold warm-start via --init_from_dir. Evidence: comb = ConcatDataset([train_ds_hard, *([train_ds_pseudo] * cfg.pseudo_repeat)]) loss = -(targets * logp) if weight is not None: loss = loss * weight[None, :].to(device)\nThe two runs share the core mechanism but not its surroundings. Before\nbeing folded into the single canonical cluster above during the offline canonicalization\nstage, the GLM 5.2 run builds substantially more machinery around it: a\nweighted soft-cross-entropy loss (pseudo-labeled samples down-weighted 0.2\nvs. 1.0 for labeled ones), pseudo-label sharpening (raising soft\nprobabilities to a power \\(>1\\) and renormalizing to peak the target\ndistribution), iterative multi-round pseudo-labeling (retraining on a\nprevious round’s ensemble output, then keeping whichever round scored better\nper model on OOF), and two rounds of brute-force combinatorial search –\nfirst over which round/resolution each ensemble member should use (up to 324\ncombinations, each scored by out-of-fold threshold-optimized QWK), then over\n7 candidate final ensemble configurations. The Kimi K3 run instead builds a\nleaner version of the same core loop, adding per-fold warm-starting\n(--init_from_dir) rather than the GLM 5.2 run’s iterative-round\nsearch. Both runs independently rediscover the same underlying technique –\nreflected in Figure [4](#S5.F4) as a rare (0.9) but\nrecurring point in Malena’s rarity distribution – while differing in how\nmuch extra engineering each backbone invests around it.\nAppendix F Contamination check\nEvery fixed30 task is a public Kaggle competition, and MLE-bench itself ships a set of reference kernels/notebooks for a subset of its competitions (public solutions collected as part of the benchmark’s own construction). We check for memorized/copied code using a vendored copy of Dolos, MLE-bench’s own source-code similarity tool, against a curated corpus of 1,350 distinct real solutions spanning all 30 fixed30 tasks.\nCode similarity (Dolos). Dolos’s default pairwise similarity score\nnormalizes shared token \\(k\\)-grams by the union of both files’ tokens (a Jaccard-style\nmeasure), which is a poor fit for our setting and, notably, biased in the direction of\nunder-reporting contamination rather than over-reporting it: Malena’s solution\nfiles are typically much smaller than the monolithic Kaggle notebooks in the real corpus,\nso even if Malena fully reused a small fragment of a large real file verbatim, the\nshared tokens would be a small fraction of the (large) union and the similarity score\nwould stay low – the metric would fail to flag exactly the kind of partial copying we\ncare about. We instead score every pair by containment – the fraction of\nthe smaller-file’s tokens that also appear in the other file, together with the\nlongest matching token run – which asks “how much of this file was drawn from the\nother”, independent of how large the other file is, and is sensitive to exactly the\nsmall-fragment-fully-reused case that raw similarity would miss. We compare\nMalena-vs-real containment against a real-vs-real cross-label null baseline (distinct,\nindependently-written real solutions, pooled across the whole 30-task corpus rather than\nrestricted to same-task pairs – see below for why). Table [22](#A6.T22)\nreports the containment distribution for both, across all 924,750 Malena-vs-real pairs\nand 473,850 real-vs-real cross-label pairs (every Malena checkpoint / every real\nsolution, pooled across all 30 tasks – i.e. including task-mismatched pairs, not just\nsame-task ones – exhaustive): Malena-vs-real containment sits below real-vs-real\ncontainment at every percentile shown. A pair is flagged only if it exceeds both\nthe real-vs-real containment and longest-match-run 95th percentiles;\nzero of the 924,750 pairs are flagged. This is the pattern we would expect\nfrom independently-written code, not from copied or memorized solutions. (The embedding\ncheck below restricts to same-task pairs only, to keep the \\(O(n^{2})\\) embedding-similarity\ncost tractable; the two pair counts are therefore not directly comparable.)\nEmbedding similarity (bag-of-chunks). As a second, token-independent check, we\nembed both corpora with Qwen3-Embedding-8B ([Zhang et al., 2025](#bib.bib49)), which is a 4096-dim embedding model, splitting each file into a sliding\nwindow of chunks (since many solution files exceed the embedding model’s context window)\nand representing each solution as a bag of chunk embeddings rather than a single vector.\nWe compare bags with three metrics: mean-pooling cosine (cosine similarity of the\ntwo bags’ mean embeddings); Chamfer similarity (for each chunk in one bag, its max\ncosine similarity to any chunk in the other bag, averaged over the bag, symmetrized by\naveraging both directions); and Chamfer with background subtraction (the raw\nChamfer score minus a background level – the median Chamfer score over 5,000 random\nMalena-vs-real chunk-bag pairs drawn from different tasks, 0.504 – to remove the\ngeneric code/library-idiom similarity any two unrelated files share). We compute all three\nover every same-task Malena-vs-real pair (62,207 pairs) and every same-task\nreal-vs-real cross-label pair (33,465 pairs), exhaustively, across all 30 tasks.\nTable [22](#A6.T22) reports the percentiles alongside the Dolos\nnumbers above. At the median (and, for mean-pooling cosine, up to the 90th percentile),\nMalena-vs-real similarity is comparable to the real-vs-real baseline rather than below\nit; but at the percentiles where copied or memorized content would actually show up –\nthe upper tail, p95 and p99 – Malena-vs-real similarity is consistently below\nthe real-vs-real baseline for all three metrics, i.e. there is no anomalously heavy right\ntail. This is the same qualitative conclusion as the Dolos check, from an independent,\ntoken-free signal.\n| metric | group | p50 | p90 | p95 | p99 | \n|---|---|---|---|---|---|\n| Dolos containment | real-vs-real | 0.008 | 0.077 | 0.143 | 0.453 | \n| Dolos containment | Malena-vs-real | 0.002 | 0.009 | 0.013 | 0.020 | \n| mean-pooling cosine | real-vs-real | 0.735 | 0.877 | 0.909 | 0.968 | \n| mean-pooling cosine | Malena-vs-real | 0.773 | 0.880 | 0.896 | 0.919 | \n| Chamfer | real-vs-real | 0.634 | 0.741 | 0.778 | 0.882 | \n| Chamfer | Malena-vs-real | 0.638 | 0.713 | 0.726 | 0.746 | \n| Chamfer (bg-subtracted) | real-vs-real | 0.130 | 0.237 | 0.273 | 0.377 | \n| Chamfer (bg-subtracted) | Malena-vs-real | 0.134 | 0.209 | 0.222 | 0.242 | \nBehavioral check: environment interaction matters. A separate, model-independent\nargument against memorization comes from comparing iteration types rather than code\ndirectly: if Malena’s solutions were largely memorized from pretraining, a single\ncompletion asked to produce a full solution to a competition it has “seen” should score\nclose to the full agentic loop, since no environment feedback would be needed to recall a\nmemorized answer.\nWe can clearly observe that such thing is not the case in our corpus.\nFrom Figure [2](#S4.F2), we can observe that even Best-of-N Oneshot iterations aggregated at 6h time budgets performs significantly better than the single Chat iteration.\nThis is the opposite of what contamination would predict, and is consistent with the agent doing real iterative\nproblem-solving rather than recalling known answers.\nAppendix G Discussion\nG.1 Limitations\nThe primary limitation of this study is that most of our results rely on\nMLE-bench ([Chan et al., 2025](#bib.bib5)), which has known issues in its\ntask preparation scripts and, being built from public Kaggle competitions, is a plausible\ntarget for pretraining contamination. We mitigate this in three ways. First, we identified\nand fixed the subset of fixed30 tasks with broken preparation scripts (missing data\nor ground-truth leakage) rather than excluding them, so that our comparisons stay on the\nfull 30-task set used elsewhere in the paper; see Appendix [A.1](#A1.SS1) and\nTable [3](#A1.T3) for which tasks were fixed. Second, we directly checked for contamination via\ntwo independent methods, a code-similarity (Dolos) check and a bag-of-embeddings\nsimilarity check, both against a curated corpus of real fixed30 solutions and both finding\nno anomalous similarity between Malena-generated code and real solutions (Appendix [F](#A6)).\nThird, we additionally evaluate on NatureBench ([Wang et al., 2026b](#bib.bib43))\n(Appendix [A.3](#A1.SS3)), whose tasks are recent open scientific-research questions\nwith no public solution corpus to memorize, though there we could only afford a smaller\nnumber of models and fewer methods than on fixed30.\nA second limitation is statistical power. Some of our results still have confidence\nintervals wide enough that we cannot make strong claims about the size of an effect; we\nbelieve our weaker claims of Malena being no worse than a given baseline are well\nsupported (Appendix [A.5](#A1.SS5) reports the paired-comparison methodology this\nrelies on), but stronger claims about exactly how much better should be read with this in\nmind. This is a direct consequence of cost: running agents for the full 24-hour budget is\nexpensive, so we could only afford 3–4 seeds per task at 24 hours, versus 6–10 seeds per\ntask for our 6-hour and completion-only conditions.\nA third limitation is scope on coordination between multiple agent workers. Our claim\nthat most harness interventions add little on top of a plain coding agent (Section [4](#S4))\nis made almost entirely with a single worker; we do run one coordination ablation\n(Section [4.4](#S4.SS4)) across a small set of coordination primitives on top of\nmultiple parallel workers, but we do not otherwise invest in coordination research\nbetween multiple concurrent streams of work. We defer this to future work, since it is\nits own interesting problem, plausibly with a different set of useful interventions than\nthe single-worker setting, and one that likely requires an even larger compute budget\nthan what we use here to study properly.\nA fourth limitation is possible asymmetry in tuning effort: because Malena is our own\nsystem, it is natural to worry that we invested disproportionately more debugging and\nconfiguration effort into it than into the external harnesses it is compared against,\ninflating its apparent advantage. We mitigate this by treating every harness’s backbone\nport the same way we treat our own: each external harness went through its own\nbackbone-specific verification pass before its canonical batch was launched, which\nsurfaced and required fixing genuine incompatibility bugs in the harnesses’ own code\n(e.g., independent tool-calling failures in both MLEvolve and ScienceFlow specific to\nGLM 5.2), and beyond bug-fixing we additionally ran targeted configuration ablations on\nScienceFlow and Arbor (worker count, resource-admission\npolicy, executor timeout, and early-stopping aggressiveness) to check whether their\ncanonical settings were leaving performance on the table.\nAppendix [D.3](#A4.SS3) reports this work in full; none of these\nablations moved outcomes by more than run-to-run noise at our sample sizes, so we kept\neach harness’s original canonical configuration, but we surface the negative results\nhere rather than reporting only the positive tuning story we have for our own system.\nG.2 Broader Outlook\nBeyond the direct ablation results, our observations across backbones and prior harnesses point at two related hypotheses about why scaffolding is losing its edge, which we lay out here as motivation for future work rather than claims this paper establishes.\nPost-training ate the harness. Frontier LLMs are increasingly post-trained directly inside a coding-agent loop – rewarded for planning, executing, and debugging across many turns of real tool use – rather than only on single-turn completions. We hypothesize that this kind of post-training substitutes for exactly the scaffolding that a plain coding-agent environment (Oneshot) would otherwise need on top of a chat completion (Chat): search over what to try next, recovery from a failed step, deciding when a result is good enough to stop on. If so, the Chat-vs-Oneshot gap (Section [4.1](#S4.SS1)) should track how much of this coding-agent post-training a model has received, more than it tracks raw model scale. We see suggestive evidence for this in the DeepSeek V4 family: the Preview releases show close to no gap between Chat and Oneshot performance, while the final releases – built on comparable base models but with substantially more coding-agent post-training – open up a clear gap, though still narrower than the gap we measure for GLM 5.2 (Section [4.1](#S4.SS1)). If this pattern holds more broadly, it reframes a common motivation behind self-evolving-harness designs, where an agent is given the ability to rewrite its own scaffolding to better match its abilities ([Wang et al., 2026a](#bib.bib42)): rather than each model needing a harness custom-fit to it at inference time, post-training may already be doing this fitting once, in advance, for every downstream task the model is later pointed at.\nCoding agents as the bitter lesson for test-time inference. A second, related reading of our results is architectural rather than about training data: for models capable enough at autonomous tool use, adding hand-designed structure on top of a plain coding-agent session – explicit search trees, planner/executor splits, multi-worker coordination – has a poor and often negative return (Section [4.2](#S4.SS2)–[4.4](#S4.SS4)), while simply running more (and more capable) coding-agent post-training inside that same plain loop could potentially perform better. This is the same shape of argument Sutton makes about general-purpose search and learning methods outscaling hand-engineered domain structure as compute grows ([Sutton, 2019](#bib.bib40)), applied to test-time inference specifically: a basic coding-agent harness plus a stronger backbone tends to close the gap that a specialized harness had opened at a weaker backbone, rather than the specialized harness’s advantage compounding. We see the same qualitative pattern in our own results: weaker backbones still benefit measurably from hand-designed workflow priors like MLEvolve’s tree search, but as backbone agentic capability increases, Malena’s plain single-session harness closes that gap and matches or exceeds MLEvolve without any of its search machinery (Table [15](#A4.T15), Appendix [D.2](#A4.SS2)). Taken together with the first hypothesis, the practical implication is the same one drawn in Section [6](#S6): the harness is not where the durable advantage lives.", "segments": null}, {"item_id": "a007976d7a6dcb8c99635a7e", "source_id": "arxiv-seeds", "publisher_id": "research-team-2610.10491", "kind": "feed", "title": "Insights from Autoresearch for Solar Panel Segmentation", "url": "https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.10491", "published_at": "2026-10-07T17:45:31Z", "retrieved_at": "2026-10-14T03:28:21.258838Z", "media_url": null, "discovery_method": "direct_metadata_fallback", "version_id": "22bcded05aecba83c6a4594604bdc843c3061c7ccdf19d4be43c9ea27beaec4f", "text_path": "data/assets/a007976d7a6dcb8c99635a7e/22bcded05aecba83c6a4594604bdc843c3061c7ccdf19d4be43c9ea27beaec4f/text.txt", "segments_path": null, "text": "Insights from Autoresearch for Solar Panel Segmentation\nAbstract\nThis paper investigates AutoResearch, a protocol in which a coding language\nmodel edits a training program under a one-hour GPU budget and retains a change\nonly if validation IoU improves.\nThe protocol is applied to photovoltaic panel segmentation on a frozen\nreal-image split, with DeepLabV3–ResNet-50 held fixed.\nThree campaigns of 24 experiments, using Gemma 4 12B, Qwen3-8B all improve their one-hour baselines, but retained modifications do\nnot transfer across hardware.\nThe Qwen3-8B configuration, trained on real images only, reaches a test IoU of\n0.836 versus 0.833 for the reference GAN-augmented schedule. Research repository [https://github.com/VU-AIML/automl4eo-autoresearch-segmentation](https://github.com/VU-AIML/automl4eo-autoresearch-segmentation).\n1 Introduction\nEarth observation (EO) segmentation is experimentally sensitive.\nLoss functions, learning-rate schedules, optimizers, and augmentations interact\nwith class imbalance, ground sampling distance (GSD), and the available GPU\nmemory. Loss function varies from vision-language models [Komurcu and Petkevicius (2025)](#bib.bib5) to recent I-JEPA approaches [Kömürcü and Petkevicius (2026)](#bib.bib6).\nPublished training configurations are often sized for a hardware platform and\nepoch budget that differ from those available in a later study.\nAs a result, transferring a reported configuration is not always effective, and\nmanual search of the design space is time-consuming.\nAutoResearch ([Karpathy, 2025](#bib.bib7)) is not a new segmentation\narchitecture.\nIt is a constrained autonomous experiment loop: a coding agent may edit only\nthe training program, runs a trial under a fixed wall-clock budget, retains the\nchange if a scalar validation metric improves, and otherwise reverts. This approach is the continuation of the field, as automated approaches were tried in [Yamada et al. (2025)](#bib.bib1); [Becktepe et al. (2025)](#bib.bib2); [Wąsala et al. (2025)](#bib.bib3); [Opdam (2025)](#bib.bib4).\nThe original implementation of autoresearch targets single-GPU language-model pretraining, with\na budget of about 300 s per idea and validation bits-per-byte as the\nacceptance criterion.\nWe argue that the same control protocol is a natural AutoML operator for EO\nsegmentation, provided that the data split and the evaluator remain outside the\nagent’s control. At each step the coding model sees the current training program, the parseable run footer, and a short instruction file that states the IoU keep/revert rule and the frozen files; it then proposes a free-form code edit rather than a point in a pre-declared search space.\nThe experimental testbed is the photovoltaic (PV) panel segmentation setting of\n[Lekavičius and Gružauskas (2024)](#bib.bib8): DeepLabV3 ([Chen et al., 2017](#bib.bib9)) with a ResNet-50\nbackbone ([He et al., 2016](#bib.bib10)), images resampled to \\(0.1\\) m/pixel and\n\\(512\\times 512\\), and a 2048/256/256 split of real images.\nThat work improved test intersection-over-union (IoU) from 0.801 without\naugmentation to 0.833 by adding 1228 pix2pix ([Isola et al., 2017](#bib.bib11)) images and\ntraining for up to 100 epochs on an A100 GPU.\nIn the present study the architecture and split are frozen, the synthetic\nimages are withheld, and AutoResearch searches only the training program under\na one-hour budget.\nThe contributions of this paper are threefold.\nFirst, the AutoResearch protocol is ported from language-model pretraining to\nbinary PV segmentation, with an immutable evaluator and a fixed real-image\nsplit.\nSecond, three independent 24-experiment campaigns are reported on two GPU\nclasses, and the retained modifications are compared.\nThird, the best Qwen3-8B configuration is evaluated once on the held-out test\nsplit and placed next to the reference Table 5 numbers of\n[Lekavičius and Gružauskas (2024)](#bib.bib8).\n2 Method\nThe preparation program owns the canonical real split, GSD resampling, object-centric \\(512\\times 512\\) crops, ImageNet normalization, and the evaluation function (mean per-image IoU on the 256-image validation set). The F1 score is logged but is not the acceptance metric. The agent never trains on validation or test images and cannot edit the evaluator. Training is launched only through a wrapper that unloads the coding model, enforces the wall-clock budget, and prints a parseable footer.\nThe agent may edit only the training program (loss, optimizer, schedule, auxiliary head, gradient clipping, and train-time augmentation). The architecture remains DeepLabV3–ResNet-50. Let \\(c_{t}\\) denote the current retained training program and \\(m(c)\\) the validation IoU after 3600 s. A proposed edit \\(\\Delta_{t}\\) is committed if \\(m(c_{t}+\\Delta_{t})>m(c_{t})\\); otherwise the tree is reset to \\(c_{t}\\). Failed runs are treated as rejections. Each campaign is capped at 24 logged experiments, including the baseline. Under a frozen architecture and evaluator the loop is close to code-level hyperparameter search; its extra degree of freedom is that proposals need not lie in a pre-specified space.\n3 Experimental study\nThe data follow the reference paper: 640 image–mask pairs from each of four\nGSDs (PV08 0.8 m, PV03 0.3 m, IGN 0.2 m, PV01+Google 0.1 m)\n([Jiang et al., 2021](#bib.bib12); [Kasmi et al., 2023](#bib.bib13)), split 512/64/64 per GSD, with random seed 35.\nOnly real images are used; the 1228 pix2pix extras are never loaded.\nThree independent campaigns are run on this split (Table [1](#S3.T1)).\n|  | Loop A | Loop B | Loop C | \n|---|---|---|---|\n| Coding model | Gemma 4 12B | Qwen3-8B | Qwen3-8B | \n| GPU | RTX 4070 12 GB | ASUS Ascent GX10 | ASUS Ascent GX10 | \n| Batch / peak VRAM | 2 / \\(\\approx\\)9 GB | 8 / 18–35 GB | 8\\(\\to\\)16 / 35–36 GB | \n| Baseline val. IoU | 0.616 | 0.723 | 0.737 | \n| Best val. IoU | 0.710 | 0.858 | 0.858 | \n| \\(\\Delta\\) val. IoU | \\(+0.094\\) | \\(+0.135\\) | \\(+0.120\\) | \n| Retained / rejected | 3 / 21 | 10 / 14 | 7 / 17 | \n| Best test IoU / F1 | — | 0.836 / 0.891 | — | \nLoop A is executed under a 12 GB memory constraint (batch size 2). Loops B and C use a cached \\(512\\times 512\\) loader on GX10-class hardware (Loop C later retains batch sizes 12 and 16). Disk caching of the resampled pairs is a harness constant, not an agent proposal. The test split is scored once, after Loop B, on the best validation checkpoint.\n3.1 Retained modifications\nAs we can see in Figure [1](#S3.F1) overlays the three traces.\nSolid lines show the running best of each campaign, i.e. the selected\nconfiguration trajectory.\nRejected trials are drawn transparently; stars mark the selected best of each\ncampaign.\nIn Loop A, a cosine schedule with 5% linear warmup increases validation IoU\nfrom 0.616 to 0.708.\nReducing Adam weight decay from \\(10^{-4}\\) to \\(10^{-5}\\) then yields 0.710.\nDice, AdamW, auxiliary BCE, gradient clipping, geometric and photometric\naugmentation, polynomial decay, and encoder freezing are rejected.\nGradient-norm clipping at 1.0 reduces IoU to 0.349, which lies below the range\nof Figure [1](#S3.F1).\nLoop B retains nine modifications after the baseline.\nCombining per-image soft Dice with BCE ([Milletari et al., 2016](#bib.bib15)) is the largest\ngain (\\(+0.103\\)).\nA polynomial schedule over the remaining wall clock then replaces the\nreference StepLR with step size 20 (\\(+0.012\\)), which would otherwise reduce the\nlearning rate after approximately 20 of the \\(\\approx\\)32 epochs that fit in one\nhour.\nSmaller retained changes follow: DeepLab auxiliary logits with weight 0.4,\nhorizontal flipping, AdamW ([Loshchilov and Hutter, 2017](#bib.bib14)), 180 s warmup,\ngradient-norm clipping at 1.0, BCE positive class weight 2, and vertical\nflipping (\\(+0.0001\\)).\nLoop C, an independent GX10 campaign, also begins with Dice (\\(+0.076\\)) and then diverges. It retains flipping together with Dice, a lower peak learning rate, a larger batch, and finally a Dice-heavy configuration with color jitter at batch size 16 (validation IoU 0.858). Unlike Loop B, it does not retain AdamW, polynomial decay, an auxiliary head, or gradient clipping.\nTable [2](#S3.T2) compares overlapping families of modifications.\nChanges retained in Loop B are often rejected in Loops A and C, or are not\nproposed.\nDice is the only large, repeated retention on GX10-class hardware; it\ndecreases IoU on the 4070 baseline.\nCosine decay is the only large retention under 12 GB and is rejected in both\nGX10 campaigns.\nHorizontal flipping, color jitter, and a lower peak learning rate change sign\ndepending on the configuration on which they are stacked.\nRotation, encoder freezing, and exponential moving averages of the weights are\nrejected in every campaign that tries them.\nThe two GX10 campaigns reach the same validation IoU of 0.858 with different\nretained sequences.\nThis coincidence is consistent with a one-hour performance plateau on this\nbackbone and split, rather than with a unique AutoML default.\n| Modification family | Loop A | Loop B | Loop C | \n|---|---|---|---|\n| Soft Dice (+BCE) | rejected | retained \\(+0.103\\) | retained \\(+0.076\\) | \n| Cosine schedule | retained \\(+0.092\\) | rejected | rejected | \n| Polynomial LR 0.9 | rejected | retained \\(+0.012\\) | — | \n| AdamW vs. Adam | rejected | retained \\(+0.005\\) | — | \n| Auxiliary head \\(\\times 0.4\\) | rejected | retained \\(+0.004\\) | — | \n| Gradient clip 1.0 | rejected \\(-0.359\\) | retained \\(+0.002\\) | — | \n| Horizontal flip | rejected | retained \\(+0.005\\) | rejected, then retained | \n| Lower peak LR | rejected \\(3{\\times}10^{-4}\\) | rejected | retained | \n| Larger batch | — (12 GB) | — | retained 8\\(\\to\\)12\\(\\to\\)16 | \n| Color jitter | rejected | rejected | retained \\(+0.001\\) | \n| Rotation / freeze / EMA | rejected | rejected | rejected | \nDiminishing returns are visible in all three traces. After the first one or two retained changes, later accepted deltas are of the order of \\(10^{-3}\\) IoU, which is within single-run variation that the protocol does not estimate.\n3.2 Comparison with the reference schedule\nTable [3](#S3.T3) places Loop B on the same frozen 256-image test\nidentifiers as Table 5 of [Lekavičius and Gružauskas (2024)](#bib.bib8).\nThe comparison is not matched for compute: the reference trains for up to 100\nepochs with early stopping on an A100 (batch size 48) and, in gan60,\nadds 1228 synthetic images.\nLoop B trains for 3600 s on 2048 real images, without test-time flipping and\nwithout a change of backbone.\nLoops A and C are validation-only.\nOn this protocol, the one-hour real-only configuration improves IoU by 0.034 and F1 by 0.038 relative to no_aug, and IoU by 0.002 and F1 by 0.011 relative to gan60. The gap between validation and test IoU is approximately 0.022, so neither GX10 validation figure of 0.858 should be read as a test result. The comparison is specific to this split, backbone, and GPU; it is not evidence that AutoResearch outperforms GAN augmentation in general.\n[Lekavičius and Gružauskas (2024)](#bib.bib8)on the same 256 test images. Loops A and C are validation-only.\n| Setup | Data | Budget | IoU | F1 | \n|---|---|---|---|---|\n| Paper no_aug | real | \\(\\leq\\)100 ep. (A100) | 0.801 | 0.853 | \n| Paper basic_aug | real | \\(\\leq\\)100 ep. (A100) | 0.813 | 0.865 | \n| Paper gan60 (paper best) | real + 60% GAN | \\(\\leq\\)100 ep. (A100) | 0.833 | 0.880 | \n| Loop C best (val.) | real | 3600 s (GX10) | 0.858† | — | \n| Loop B best (val.) | real | 3600 s (GX10) | 0.858† | 0.908† | \n| Loop B best (test) | real | 3600 s (GX10) | 0.836 | 0.891 | \n| †Validation split; not comparable to the paper’s test rows. |  |  |  |  | \nEach proposed change is evaluated with a single random seed (35). Loops A and C have no test evaluation. The coding model is confounded with GPU class, and the agent cannot change the backbone, the split, or the metric.\n4 Conclusion\nMotivated by the cost of manually searching EO segmentation configurations, this paper investigates AutoResearch as an AutoML operator for solar-panel segmentation. Three campaigns improve one-hour DeepLabV3 baselines on a frozen real split. The retained modifications are a cosine schedule with reduced weight decay under 12 GB, and two GX10 sequences that both reach a validation IoU of 0.858. The Qwen3-8B configuration matches the reference test IoU without synthetic images. The protocol is effective under a declared budget if the evaluator remains frozen and retained modifications are not treated as universal defaults. A direct comparison with a declared-space tuner, and a less restricted loop that may edit architecture, preprocessing, or literature-derived ideas, are left to future work.\nReferences\n-
